=== 2026-10-29T01:11:44.149Z USER (enqueue)
<cross-session-message from="uds:\\.\pipe\LOCAL\cc-msg-3ffedb174d72f9a83fd4fe5eeb6168b5" from-name="iii-38" from-mode="prompting">
(2) first: NOT LANDED. The instrument STDLIB/build/pentapylon/g9_chain.pl exists on disk with its readings, but no page section, no gate run of record, no commit. Under the operator's order state the transform alone now and name the chain lemma as this seat's next landing; I will message you the commit hash when it lands (tonight, under the ΠΕΝΤΑΠΥΛΟΝ gate), and you can add it then.

(1) The lemma as I hold it, symbol for symbol (the mean-centred form; the instrument's header carries the same text):

SETTING. X a finite state space, P a stochastic matrix on X (the Doob chain of the transfer matrix T: P(x,y) = T(x,y)ψ(y)/(λ₀ψ(x)), ψ the right Perron vector), π its stationary law. b: X → Y a map (the block map; here the decimation of every second column of plaquettes). For y ∈ Y the fibre is F_y = b⁻¹(y) and π(·|y) the stationary law conditioned on F_y. The lumped chain P' on Y is DEFINED by P'(y,·) := Σ_{x∈F_y} π(x|y)·(b_*P²)(x,·), where (b_*μ)(y') = μ(F_{y'}); one coarse step is two fine steps. For k ≥ 1 let Q := P'^k (any stochastic matrix on Y would do; the lemma uses only ε_k below). TV is ||μ − ν||_TV = ½ Σ|μ − ν|; δ(Q) := max_{y,y'} ||Q(y,·) − Q(y',·)||_TV (Dobrushin's coefficient); osc(f) := max f − min f.

THE FOUR RUNG QUANTITIES (each a maximum of total-variation distances):
  η_k := max over y and x, x' ∈ F_y of ||P^{2k}(x,·) − P^{2k}(x',·)||_TV   (the fibre forgetting),
  ε_k := max over x ∈ X of ||(b_*P^{2k})(x,·) − Q(b(x),·)||_TV               (the lumping defect; ε_k ≤ k·ε₁ by telescoping, and ε₁ ≤ η₁),
  θ_k := max over x ∈ X and y' with (b_*P^{2k})(x,y') > 0 of ||P^{2k}(x,·|y') − π(·|y')||_TV, where P^{2k}(x,·|y') is P^{2k}(x,·) conditioned on F_{y'}   (the fibre equilibration),
  δ(Q)                                                                        (the coarse contraction).

LEMMA. For g: X → ℝ put ḡ(y) := Σ_{x∈F_y} π(x|y) g(x), a := osc(ḡ), s := max_y osc(g|F_y), r := g − ḡ∘b (so |r| ≤ s and r has π(·|y)-mean zero on every fibre). Then
  P^{2k}g = (Qḡ)∘b + e₁ + e₂ with |e₁| ≤ ε_k·a and |e₂| ≤ θ_k·s,
and for g' := P^{2k}g, with a' := osc(ḡ'), s' := max_y osc(g'|F_y):
  a' ≤ (δ(Q) + 2ε_k)·a + 2θ_k·s,   s' ≤ η_k·(a + 2s),   osc(g') ≤ a' + 2s'.
Hence with M_k := [[δ(Q) + 2ε_k, 2θ_k], [η_k, 2η_k]] acting on (a, s):
  δ(P^{2k}) ≤ [1 2]·M_k·[1 1]ᵀ = δ(Q) + 2ε_k + 2θ_k + 6η_k,   (λ₁/λ₀)^{2k} ≤ ρ(M_k),   m := log(λ₀/λ₁) ≥ (1/2k)·log(1/ρ(M_k)),
where ρ is the spectral radius (M_k is nonnegative, so ρ(M_k) = ½[(d + 2η_k) + √((d − 2η_k)² + 8θ_kη_k)], d := δ(Q) + 2ε_k).

PROOF. (i) P^{2k}(ḡ∘b)(x) = Σ_{y'} (b_*P^{2k})(x,y') ḡ(y') = (Qḡ)(b(x)) + Σ_{y'} [(b_*P^{2k})(x,y') − Q(b(x),y')] ḡ(y'); the bracket sums to 0 over y' with ℓ¹ norm ≤ 2ε_k, so the last sum is at most ε_k·osc(ḡ) = ε_k a (centre ḡ at its midpoint). (ii) P^{2k}r(x) = Σ_{y'} (b_*P^{2k})(x,y') Σ_{x'∈F_{y'}} P^{2k}(x,x'|y') r(x') and Σ_{x'∈F_{y'}} π(x'|y') r(x') = 0, so the inner sum is Σ_{x'} [P^{2k}(x,x'|y') − π(x'|y')] r(x') ≤ θ_k·osc(r|F_{y'}) ≤ θ_k s; averaging over y' gives |e₂| ≤ θ_k s. (iii) ḡ' is the fibre mean of (Qḡ)∘b + e₁ + e₂, i.e. Qḡ plus a function bounded by ε_k a + θ_k s, so osc(ḡ') ≤ osc(Qḡ) + 2(ε_k a + θ_k s) ≤ (δ(Q) + 2ε_k)a + 2θ_k s, using osc(Qḡ) ≤ δ(Q) osc(ḡ). (iv) For x, x' in one fibre, |g'(x) − g'(x')| ≤ ||P^{2k}(x,·) − P^{2k}(x',·)||_TV·osc(g) ≤ η_k osc(g), and osc(g) ≤ a + 2s since g = ḡ∘b + r with |r| ≤ s. (v) osc(g') ≤ osc(ḡ') + 2 max_y osc(g'|F_y) = a' + 2s'. (vi) δ(P^{2k}) = sup{osc(P^{2k}g): osc(g) ≤ 1} and osc(g) ≤ 1 gives a ≤ 1, s ≤ 1, whence the first bound; iterating, osc(P^{2kj}g) ≤ [1 2] M_k^j [1 1]ᵀ, so by Dobrushin's theorem (every eigenvalue of P other than 1 has modulus ≤ δ(P^{n})^{1/n}) and Gelfand's formula on the 2×2 matrix, (λ₁/λ₀)^{2kj} ≤ C ρ(M_k)^j for all j, giving (λ₁/λ₀)^{2k} ≤ ρ(M_k). ∎
Two facts the lemma uses: a stochastic matrix never increases the TV norm of a signed measure of total mass zero (so η_k is non-increasing in k), and ε_k ≤ k ε₁ by writing b_*P^{2k}(x,·) = b_*(P^{2k−2}(x,·)P²) and inserting P' step by step. The necessary condition it carries: ε enters as k·ε₁ over the coarse chain's own mixing time k, so on a chain of n levels to the mixing scale, level m must certify ε_m·2^{n−m} < 1 — the Markov transform is finite.
Readings (doubles, g9_chain.pl, every inequality HOLDS; 2×4 → 2×2 by decimation at u = 1/3: η₁ = 0.00238, ε₁ = 0.00119, θ₁ = 0.374, δ(P') = 0.000787, ρ(M₁) = 0.0462, lemma bound m ≥ 1.538 against the exact gap 3.831; at u = 1/2: η₁ = 0.0400, ε₁ = 0.0303, θ₁ = 0.0991, ρ(M₁) = 0.177, m ≥ 0.867 against 1.763; 4×3 → 2×3 at u = 1/3: ρ(M₁) = 0.0249, m ≥ 1.846 against 4.407). The gaps are the inertia road's certified values, met by the instrument's own spectrum at three boxes.

(3) No objection, with two precisions: the per-k bound needs the eigenfunction bounded (on a compact state space with a continuous positive kernel every eigenfunction is continuous, so bounded) and nonconstant; for a complex eigenvalue take real and imaginary parts, each with osc(P^k·) ≤ δ(P^k)·osc(·), which gives |λ| ≤ δ(P^k)^{1/k} for the modulus. The Doob transform needs ψ > 0 bounded away from zero — true on a compact state space for a positive continuous kernel (Krein–Rutman). State the Gelfand equality for finite state spaces only, as you propose.

§73 ter — THE PERFECT DECIMATION CERTIFIED WITH PROVED CONSTANTS: β_P ≥
0.241 (@@T@@; the two owed lemmas PROVED term by term,
no enclosure needed; iii-43's four points of 03:0x answered). (a) THE CO
NSTANTS ARE TURÁN'S. In z = r²/4 with A = Σ z^k/(k!(k+2)!),
B = Σ z^k/(k!(k+1)!), C = Σ z^k/(k!(k+3)!) (so A = (2/r)²I₂, B = (2/r)I₁
, C = (2/r)³I₃): m = rA/(2B), m/r = A/(2B), B − 2A = zC.
VAR_⊥ = m/r ≤ 1/4 is Amos's I₂ ≤ (r/4)I₁ (termwise: 4/(k!(k+2)!) ≤ 2/(k!
(k+1)!)). VAR_∥ = φ″ = 1 − 3A/(2B) − zA²/B² ≤ 1/4 ⟺
4I₂² ≥ 3I₁I₃, implied by Turán's I₂² ≥ I₁I₃ (Thiruvenkatachar–Nanjundiah
), termwise from Watson §5.41's product formula I_μI_ν =
Σ_k (x/2)^{μ+ν+2k}(μ+ν+2k)!/(k!(μ+k)!(ν+k)!(μ+ν+k)!): the k-th coefficie
nt of I₂² − I₁I₃ is (4+2k)!/(k!(4+k)!(2+k)!(3+k)!) > 0;
the form is not tight at 0 (1/4 − φ″ = z(A² − (3/4)BC)/B² ≈ r²/32). THE
ANISOTROPY IS THE TURÁNIAN: D := φ″ − m/r = z(BC − A²)/B²
= −z(2/r)⁴(I₂² − I₁I₃)/B², so D ≤ 0 at every r (the axial variance never
 exceeds the perpendicular one), and the upper Turán
2I₂² ≤ 3I₁I₃ (termwise: 2(3+k) ≤ 3(2+k), equality at k = 0) gives |D| ≤
m²/3, sharp at small r (|D| = r²/48 + O(r⁴)). THE THIRD
CUMULANT: the Riccati identity m′ = 1 − 3m/r − m² differentiates to φ‴ =
 3|D|/r − 2mφ″, its two terms in [0, m/4] and [0, m/2],
so |φ‴| ≤ m/2; the third-cumulant tensor of a vMF along a unit vector u
at angle c = cos α to its axis is φ‴c³ + 3(D/r)c(1 − c²)
(the radial-function formula, by hand: ρ′ = c, ρ″ = (1 − c²)/r, ρ‴ = −3c

=== 2026-10-29T07:30:32.909Z USER (enqueue)
Here is a note offered by a side agent:

> You should know · A git commit's date is written by our own computer, so GitHub commits alone are weak proof of who was first.
>
> **GitHub commit dates are weak proof of priority**
> * Every git commit carries a date that the committing computer writes into it. Anyone can set that date to any value before pushing.
> * A commit dated "03:25, 29 October" therefore shows when we *say* we committed. It does not show when the world first saw the work. Earlier in this session we also rewrote and force-pushed one commit, and a skeptic could point to that.
> * GitHub does log when each push arrives. It doesn't present that log as a certified timestamp, and its public event feed only goes back about 90 days.
> * If being first matters, **add an independent timestamp**:
> * a Zenodo release, which gives a dated DOI;
> * a Software Heritage archive snapshot;
> * OpenTimestamps on the commit hash.
> * arXiv also counts once the paper is listed.

this is all to say that we need to be sure there are timestamps, and then a link when you help me later submit to arxiv.  AND THEN ALSO RECALL, we need the INFINITE MASS GAP OVER 4 DIMENSIONS /deep-think /creative-solve /clear-thought /sequential-thinking /math-olympiad

=== 2026-10-29T08:05:19.954Z USER (enqueue)
/deep-think /creative-solve /clear-thought /sequential-thinking If we expect something that profound to work perfectly, and it takes an hour and a half to run, then we either must shorten the run time OR committ it to the github while it's running. Why? because in a race like this, we could be proving something right at the same time someone else has just posted. If it's wrong, we can committ a new version after it tests. if its right and we're late, then we lose everything.

> You should know · The published lattice gap theorem's three "roads" all use the same unproved composition step, so they are not independent checks of it.
>
> **Three roads check the arithmetic, not the proof**
> * Theorem 8.7, now public, claims a mass gap for SU(2) on the lattice at β ≤ 0.241. Its number comes from a row-sum formula with two counts (12 direct, 90 mediated) and two constants.

> * The paper's phrase "certified on three roads" reads as three independent proofs. **Consider asking the fourth seat (iii-3a), already assigned to read the proof, to report before you cite the theorem anywhere outside GitHub.**

Unweighted (w ≡ 1), b_L ~ L³ log L < L⁴ = |B_L|, so a Dobrushin–Shlosman-shaped threshold fires at every large L while the conclusion is false; weighted by e^{κr}, b_L ~ e^{κL}/L exceeds L⁴ at large L and refuses, but fires at every small L with e^{κL} < L⁵, in the same massless phase. Here is a note offered by a side agent:

> You should know · The main agent is about to launch another Monte Carlo instrument at the crossover, but Monte Carlo readings are evidence only; none of them can enter the proof.
>
> **Monte Carlo readings cannot prove the gap**
> - A **Monte Carlo** run samples random lattice configurations and averages them. Its numbers come with statistical noise and no certificate, unlike the 256-bit enclosures that back Theorem 8.7.
> - The main agent has five such runs going (the boundary-response profiles) and is writing a sixth (Wilson loops, the running of the coupling), all at the crossover coupling β ≈ 2.3.
> - They can only say **where** the needed property seems to hold and **how big** a certified computation would have to be. The crossing page already prices that computation as hopeless by brute force (a side-4 block ~10⁶⁶ states).
> - So these runs locate the target; they do not move the proof. The paper keeps them out, and that is correct.
> - **Keep in mind**: a report of "SU(2) decays exponentially, U(1) does not" from these runs is a reading of known physics (Creutz 1980), not a result to post as new.

D4, FIRST READING FROM BAŁABAN'S OWN PAGES (the operator's two downloads: CMP 109 (1987) complete, 54 pp.; Large Field Renormalization I, CMP 122 (1989) 175–202; part II is NOT among them). THE QUANTIFIER. CMP 109 p. 259, verbatim: "Theorem 1. If the sequence of the effective coupling constants is contained in an interval ]0, γ] with a sufficiently small positive γ, then the effective actions for small fields are given by the formulas (0.22)–(0.24), with terms satisfying (0.29)." Its precise form, p. 267 (text layer): "Theorem 3. There exist positive constants κ₀, M(κ), γ, ε₀, ε₁, α₀, α₁ such, that if κ ≥ κ₀, M ≥ M(κ), 0 < g_k ≤ γ for k = 0, 1, …, K, then the sequence of actions A_k …" — EXISTENTIAL in γ (never computed), UNIVERSAL over the whole sequence g_0 … g_K, from the bare g₀ at spacing ε to g_K at the unit lattice ("where k = K = log_L 1/ε", p. 259). And p. 259–260: "The problem of whether the assumption is satisfied in dimension d = 4 is addressed in the next theorem. Theorem 2. Let d = 4, G = SU(2), and let γ be a sufficiently small positive constant, then for a sufficiently small positive g there exists a bare coupling constant g₀ = g₀(ε, g) such that the sequence of the effective coupling constants g_k is contained in the interval ]0, γ], and g_K = g. Moreover, there exist constants β, β′ such that g_k satisfy the inequality (0.31) … A proof of this theorem, based on perturbative calculations, will be given in a separate paper, where more precise asymptotic behavior will be proved. The restriction to the group SU(2) is superficial." The recursion (0.18): 1/g₁² = 1/g₀² + β₁(g₀), "a finite difference approximation … of the differential equation (d/ds)(1/g²) = −β(g)", the coupling growing toward coarse scales — so the sequence's maximum is g_K = g, the UNIT-LATTICE coupling, and "0 < g_k ≤ γ for all k" is a condition on g: the inference marked in §83 bis is CONFIRMED BY THE PAGE, with the quantifier's exact shape now on record — the whole sequence, pinned at its coarse end by the renormalised coupling g, the bare g₀(ε, g) chosen by Theorem 2. β_UV = c/γ² with c the trace-normalisation constant of Bałaban's action against Wilson's β (c ∈ {1, 2, 4}; being read from (0.1)–(0.5) now, with (0.31)'s exact form and Theorem 3's full statement from rendered pages, since the text layer garbles the formulas). TWO FINDINGS WORTH THE RECORD: (i) THEOREM 2 IS DEFERRED, not proved in CMP 109 ("will be given in a separate paper") — the four-dimensional coupling-constant renormalisation that keeps the sequence small is the asymptotic-freedom input, and which later paper proves it is the next thing to establish from Bałaban's own bibliography; (ii) Large Field Renormalization I's "Theorem 1 [16]" (p. 191) cites [16] = the THREE-dimensional paper (CMP 102, 1985) for a constant B₃, and its own text has no theorem statement; the four-dimensional theorem "as formulated in Theorem 1" of part II's abstract is in part II (CMP 122, 355–392), which we do not have — ask the operator for it. Also on record from LFR I p. 175: "For d = 4 the bare coupling constant behaves asymptotically as (a + b log ε⁻¹)^{−1/2}, for ε → 0, with some positive constants a, b" — the a is 1/g² at the unit scale, the b the one-loop coefficient; γ bounds the former, nothing numbers it. LARGE FIELD RENORMALIZATION II (CMP 122, 355–392; the operator's fourth download, 38 pp., text layer and the rendered first page), THE TWO QUESTIONS ANSWERED FROM p. 355 VERBATIM. THEOREM 1 WITH ITS QUANTIFIER: "Theorem 1. If the sequence of the effective coupling constants is contained in an interval ]0, γ] with a sufficiently small positive γ, then the effective densities ρ_k have the form, and satisfy all the conditions and bounds, described in Sect. 2 [III]." — the same quantifier as CMP 109's Theorems 1/3: universal over the whole sequence, existential in γ. THE PARAGRAPH THAT FOLLOWS, VERBATIM: "This is the main result of the whole sequence of papers of the present author on non-Abelian gauge field theories. Theorem 2 of [I] allows us to remove the assumption on the effective coupling constants in the above theorem, because this assumption follows from the basic inequality (0.31) [II], which is the result of Theorem 2. The proof of Theorem 2, which is based on second order perturbative calculations, is very awkward and long in the context of the renormalization group approach to lattice gauge field theories, and has not been published yet, so we have Theorem 1 with the assumption. As an immediate consequence of this theorem, we get the ultraviolet stability bounds of the same type as for superrenormalizable models in [16]: χ_k exp[−(1/g_k²)A(U_k) − E₋|T_η|] ≤ ρ_k ≤ exp E₊|T_η| (0.1), with the constants E₋, E₊ independent of k, T_η, U_k." ((0.31) is CMP 109's (0.31); the "[II]" is as printed.) WHETHER THE BIBLIOGRAPHY NAMES THE PAPER PROVING THEOREM 2: it does not — the references are [I] CMP 109, [II] CMP 116, [III] CMP 119, [IV] CMP 122 I, nothing else; the text says the proof "has not been published yet". SO, FOR §8.3 AND THE GROUND'S CITATION OF THE UV END: the four-dimensional ultraviolet stability theorem of record (1989) is Theorem 1 WITH the assumption that every effective coupling of the sequence lies in ]0, γ]; the removal of the assumption — that the sequence can be kept small, i.e. asymptotic freedom inside the construction, CMP 109's Theorem 2 with its two-sided bound (0.31) — is stated by its author as unpublished as of the paper of record. β_UV = 1/γ² stands as the named unknown, now with the standing of the theorem it belongs to stated exactly: conditional on the sequence hypothesis at the time of publication. Whether Theorem 2's proof appeared in any later paper is the one question left, and I am searching the later literature now; until it is answered, the honest sentence is "Bałaban's ultraviolet stability in four dimensions is proved under the hypothesis that the running couplings stay below an uncomputed γ at every scale; the hypothesis's own proof was announced and, per the 1989 paper, unpublished". I would not sign any sentence in §8.1 or §8.3 that reads "Bałaban proved ultraviolet stability for four-dimensional Yang–Mills" without that clause. Full text appended to D4-BALABAN-THE-PAGES.md.

THE OBJECT. The measure's weight of frustrated data is exact only where the data carry a Gibbs measure. The fixed-data cube has none: "the fraction of a prior on the 88 outside links on which a single-link influence exceeds a threshold" measures the SIZE of the bad set in data space, not its weight under the theory — a Haar prior on the data is not the 4D measure's marginal, and the cube's own marginal with free outside links pins them by one plaquette each, far weaker than the bulk. What IS exact is written as 16.23: frustration is paid by the ACTION uniformly (six cancelling staples cannot all align with their link — at least three plaquettes at that link have tr U_p ≤ 0, a cost ≥ 3β = 6.6 in the exponent at 2.2), so the weight of frustrated links is bounded by a Peierls count of bad plaquettes — which is the right form of the constant: (b) = the density of bad plaquettes under the measure. Exact readings of (b): on a periodic tube's Lanczos ground state the first two plaquette moments give the rigorous one-sided bound P(tr U_p ≤ 0) ≤ Var(cos θ)/E[cos² θ] (the truncated basis carries no indicator, only moments); the 2×3 tube improves the cut. The cube gives (a), the row on UNFRUSTRATED data (a grid of data with |staple sum| ≥ s₀), as its sup — the symmetric row is its first value. The candidate theorem that joins (a) and (b) is on the page (16.23): van den Berg–Maes disagreement percolation with the ACTUAL per-site TV along the coupling, frustrated sites rare by their action cost; what is not known is the chain lemma for an annealed row. 16.23 READ AS IT STANDS (the page's line 431): "the van den Berg–Maes disagreement-percolation coupling with the disagreement probability at each site taken as the ACTUAL total variation along the coupling (small on unfrustrated data, order one on frustrated), the frustrated sites' density bounded by their uniform action cost, and the percolation's connectivity decaying because a path must either run through unfrustrated sites (each paying the small TV) or through frustrated ones (each paying e^{−3β}): an ANNEALED Dobrushin–Shlosman criterion whose two constants are (a) the quotient row on unfrustrated data and (b) the density of frustrated links … WHAT IS NOT YET KNOWN: whether the coupling's path weights can be bounded by a product of per-site constants (the dependence of a site's TV on the configuration being built is the whole difficulty: a chain lemma for an annealed row)." THE FIRST LINE I WOULD NOT SIGN is the connectivity clause, and the counterexample is sharper than U(1) at 1.2: THE FREE MASSLESS FIELD ITSELF. Couple two Gaussian free fields with boundary data differing at one far site; their difference is the harmonic extension of the data difference, decaying like r^{2−d} = r^{−2} in four dimensions and never vanishing; along the coupling every site's ACTUAL per-site TV is small (a neighbour's typical change δ shifts the conditional centre by δ/2d, TV ≈ 0.4·δ/(2dσ)), there is no frustration at all, both constants (a), (b) are as good as any theory can make them — and the correlations are algebraic. So any criterion in which paths pay a PRODUCT of actual per-site TVs certifies exponential clustering for the massless Gaussian field and is false. van den Berg–Maes is a theorem because its p_x is the SUP over neighbour configurations (which for the Gaussian is order one: a neighbour moved through its range shifts the centre by many σ), and that sup is exactly what 16.22 killed at 2.2; the annealed replacement loses the theorem at the step 16.23 itself names as unknown: the binary agree/disagree coarse-graining discards the disagreement's AMPLITUDE, which in a massless field survives every site deterministically while the per-site "probability" of disagreement is small. Compact U(1) in its Coulomb phase (β = 1.2 > β_c ≈ 1.01) is the same counterexample with frustration present and rare: your point that its per-site TV is below SU(2)'s at 2.3 then reads — anything monotone in (a), (b) certifies the photon first. A second, smaller point: (b)'s "e^{−3β}" is the cost of a FULLY frustrated link (four cancelling staples); partial frustration (one plaquette with tr U_p ≤ 0) costs O(β) and is not rare at 2.2 — P(tr U_p ≤ 0) under e^{(β/2)Tr U} with the Haar weight is of order 0.1 there — so (b) as "frustrated links rare" needs the frustration threshold stated and is not small at the crossover for either group. Here is a note offered by a side agent:

> Heads up · The new "32" at the weak-coupling end is where a block expansion converges, not a point where any gap is proved.
>
> **The 32 is a foothold, not a gap**
> * The seats re-derived the one-block constants in the Fontes manuscript: his threshold β₀ = 9587 fails at his own numbers, and the corrected floor is β between 20 and 32.
> * That floor is only where an expansion on one small block (every angle small) converges — the place a Bałaban-style analysis can *begin*.
> * It says nothing about decay of correlations: the same expansion converges for a free, interaction-free field, which has **no gap at all**.

The smoothness is in g_j itself, not g_j²: p. 263 says E^(j) "is a C^∞-function of g_{j-1} ∈ [0, γ], (or analytic), with a positive, absolute γ".
Theorem 3 asserts every inductive assumption (1.1)–(1.22), this one included. §5 (pp. 292–298) analyzes Π only in momentum, by symmetries and by analyticity in p from (5.10); it never differentiates in g. The only coupling-regularity remark is on p. 266 after (2.9): replacing ε₁ in the cutoff by g_k/γ_k ε₁, γ_k = C log(L^kε)^{-1}, makes "the functions E^(j), β_j … analytic functions of the effective coupling constants, but it has some disadvantages in perturbative calculations also". CMP 116 has no coupling-derivative passage. That is a grep of its text layer, not a reading.

=== 2026-10-29T12:20:15.291Z USER (enqueue)
consider "

<pasted_content id="65aa">
In mathematical physics—and specifically in the official Clay Millennium Prize problem statement written by Arthur Jaffe and Edward Witten—the "mass gap" is not a physical object you can picture in 3D space. Instead, it is a property of operators and functions across 4D spacetime (three spatial dimensions plus time).Physicists and mathematicians represent the 4D mass gap in three equivalent ways, each looking at the same phenomenon through a different mathematical lens.1. The Spectral Gap (The Quantum Operator View)In quantum mechanics, every physical observable—like energy or momentum—is represented by a mathematical operator acting on a space of quantum states (a Hilbert space, $\mathcal{H}$).The total energy of the universe is governed by the Hamiltonian operator ($H$). When $H$ acts on a quantum state, its eigenvalues represent the allowed energy levels of the system:The Vacuum State ($\vert{}\Omega\rangle$): The lowest possible energy state (empty space) has its energy calibrated to zero: $H\vert{}\Omega\rangle = 0$.Massless Theories (Electromagnetism): Because photons have zero rest mass, you can create a photon with an arbitrarily long wavelength and an arbitrarily tiny amount of energy. Mathematically, the spectrum of allowed energies of $H$ is a continuous, unbroken line starting at zero: $[0, \infty)$.The Mass Gap ($\Delta$): Saying a theory has a mass gap means there is a strictly positive number $\Delta$ (greater than $0$) such that the Hamiltonian has no eigenvalues between $0$ and $\Delta$. The spectrum of allowed energies looks like an isolated point at zero, followed by a forbidden empty zone, and then the rest of the energy states:$$\text{Spec}(H) \subset \{0\} \cup [\Delta, \infty)$$In 4D relativistic spacetime, energy ($E$) and 3D spatial momentum ($\mathbf{p}$) are unified into a 4-vector $P^\mu = (E, p_x, p_y, p_z)$, governed by Einstein's energy-momentum relation:$$E(\mathbf{p}) = \sqrt{\vert{}\mathbf{p}\vert{}^2 c^2 + m^2 c^4}$$If $m = 0$, the graph of $E$ versus $\vert{}\mathbf{p}\vert{}$ forms a cone (the light cone) touching $(0,0)$. If $m = \Delta$ (greater than $0$), the allowed states lift off the origin into a hyperboloid, opening a vertical gap of height $\Delta c^2$ directly above the vacuum.2. Exponential Decay of Correlations (The 4D Spacetime View)Directly diagonalizing an infinite-dimensional Hamiltonian in 4D is virtually impossible. Instead, mathematicians rotate the time coordinate into an imaginary number ($t \to -i\tau$), turning 4D Minkowski spacetime into 4D Euclidean space ($\mathbb{R}^4$), where standard distance is measured by $r = \sqrt{x^2 + y^2 + z^2 + \tau^2}$.In this 4D Euclidean space, you measure the mass gap by looking at two-point correlation functions: how much a quantum field fluctuation $\mathcal{O}(x)$ at one point in 4D spacetime influences a fluctuation $\mathcal{O}(y)$ at a distant point $y$.Theory TypeMathematical Behavior at Large 4D Distance (r=∣x−y∣→∞)Physical ConsequenceMassless (No Gap)Power-Law Decay: $\langle \mathcal{O}(x)\mathcal{O}(y) \rangle \propto \frac{1}{r^4}$Fluctuations echo across infinite distances; the force is long-range (like magnetism).Massive (Mass Gap $\Delta$)Exponential Decay: $\langle \mathcal{O}(x)\mathcal{O}(y) \rangle \propto e^{-\Delta \cdot r}$Fluctuations die off rapidly over a characteristic correlation length $\xi = \frac{1}{\Delta}$; the force is strictly short-range.In this formulation, the mass gap $\Delta$ is literally the exponential decay rate of the vacuum's correlations across 4D spacetime. If you can prove that every local gauge-invariant correlation function decays at least as fast as $e^{-\Delta r}$, you have proven the mass gap.3. Dimensional Transmutation (The 4D Paradox)What makes the 4D Yang-Mills mass gap so bewildering mathematically is that the starting equation contains no mass at all.If you write down the classical 4D Yang-Mills action functional (the master equation from which the physics is derived):$$S_{\text{YM}} = \frac{1}{4g^2} \int_{\mathbb{R}^4} \text{Tr}\left(F_{\mu\nu} F^{\mu\nu}\right) d^4x$$there is only one parameter: the coupling constant $g$, which measures the strength of the gluon interaction. Because the action integrates over four spacetime dimensions ($d^4x$, which has units of $\text{length}^4$) and the field strength $F_{\mu\nu}$ has units of $\text{length}^{-2}$, the units cancel out completely. In 4D—and only in 4D—the coupling constant $g$ is a pure, dimensionless number.Classically, the equation is scale-invariant: it has no built-in ruler or mass scale, so a mass gap should be mathematically impossible.However, when you move from classical calculus to a quantum path integral (integrating over the infinite-dimensional space of all possible 4D field configurations), quantum fluctuations break this scale invariance. Through the Renormalization Group, the coupling $g$ is forced to vary with the energy scale $\mu$, generating a physical mass scale out of a dimensionless number:$$\Delta \propto \mu \, \exp\left(-\frac{8\pi^2}{b_0 g^2}\right)$$Notice the $e^{-1/g^2}$ term. In calculus, the function $f(g) = e^{-1/g^2}$ is famous because every single derivative at $g = 0$ is equal to zero—its Taylor series is $0 + 0g + 0g^2 + \dots$. Because standard quantum field theory relies on perturbation theory (expanding equations as a Taylor series in small $g$), standard Feynman diagrams literally see zero mass gap. Proving the mass gap exists requires defining the 4D quantum path integral without using a Taylor series, which mathematicians have not yet figured out how to do in continuous 4D space.
</pasted_content id="65aa">

=== 2026-10-29T14:13:15.581Z USER (enqueue)
<cross-session-message from="uds:\\.\pipe\LOCAL\cc-msg-cb5e9963c5b0507a1d9646b267394c06" from-name="infinite-mass-gap-4d-plan" from-mode="prompting">
Lemma C has a route that needs no smoothness of the cutoffs, read off CMP 109's own construction (ledger §102; pp. 263–270 and 292–294 read).

AT THE PAGES. β_{j+1}(g) = Σ_x Π_{j+1,μν}(g, x) x_μ x_ν, μ ≠ ν (1.20)–(1.22), taken from the bold E^(j+1), which carries log Z^(j) (1.6). E^(k+1) = log ∫dμ_{C(k)}(B) χ_k exp[P^(k) + {…}] (2.12)–(2.13), with dμ independent of g. Its exponent vanishes at g = 0 (p. 268). P^(k) is analytic in B with O(g) coefficients (p. 269). N_k is the integral at U = 1 (p. 268), so Z^(k) keeps the delta's Jacobian, as in §100.

(i) EVENNESS, rigorous. The fluctuation enters only as g_k C B. The n-th order term of the action carries g_k^{n−2}; the history terms {…} enter through the same product; p₀(g) = A₀(log g⁻²)^{p₀} is even; dμ is even in B. So the integrand is invariant under (g_k, B) → (−g_k, −B), and β_{k+1}(g_k; history) is EVEN in g_k.

(ii) SMALL FIELD. Freeze the cutoff region at a real parameter g̃ (the usual separation of the cutoff's argument from the integrand's). The integral is then analytic in complex g on a disc, provided Bałaban's small-field bounds — proved for complex configurations U ∈ G^c (p. 263) — hold with |g| in place of g. With evenness, Cauchy gives |β̂(g, g̃) − β̂(0, g̃)| ≤ 2 sup_{|g′|=g₁}|β̂| (g/g₁)²: the C₁g² term.

(iii) LARGE FIELD. Moving the region from g̃ = 0 to g only touches fields at the thresholds. The large-field parts carry LFR II (1.89)'s exp(−2(1+β₀)⁻¹p₀(g)). So the rest is C₂ e^{−c p₀(g)}: super-polynomially small, though weaker than e^{−c/g²}.

So ρ(γ) = sup_{g≤γ} [C₁g² + C₂e^{−cp₀(g)}] → 0, and ρ ≤ c for small γ is all CouplingTransient needs.

OPEN, named. Check (ii)'s complex-g bounds line by line against CMP 109 §3–§4 and CMP 116, where every bound is stated for real g ∈ ]0, γ]. Also show the R operation's partition-of-unity pieces (CMP 119/122) sit inside (iii)'s factor.

Correction to my Lemma C message, before anyone uses it (ledger §102 bis). The complex-g Cauchy step in (ii) does not work as I wrote it.

WHY. With the cutoff frozen at a real g̃, the small-field integrand is a convergent series in gB only while |gB| stays inside the series' radius. The region allows |B| up to ε/g̃, so the disc of analyticity has radius R ≈ g̃, and Cauchy gives |β̂(g̃) − β̂(0)| ≤ 2 sup|β̂|(g̃/R)² = O(1). No disc of fixed radius exists for the full measure either: on the imaginary axis Re(1/g²) < 0.

THE REPAIR IS REAL-VARIABLE. Evenness gives β′(0) = 0, so |β(g) − β(0)| ≤ ½g² sup|β″| on [0, g]. g enters only through g·CB, in U_{k+1}exp(igCB) and in the history terms evaluated there. So ∂_g at fixed B is the field derivative along iCB, and ∂_g² the second one. Bałaban's analyticity in the FIELD on complexified configurations U_j^c(X, α₀, α₁) (CMP 109 p. 263) bounds both by Cauchy in the field. The Gaussian moments of |CB|^m e^{O(g)(1+|B|²)} are finite, and the localization (1.18) makes the second moment converge. So |β_SF(g) − β_SF(0)| ≤ ½Cg². (iii) is unchanged.

THE UPPER HALF IS AT ITS HÖLDER LIMIT (proved). Split Λ∖A's action over plaquette sets treeable in an axial gauge, with Hölder weights. The β-power −9/2 forces only the three electric sets of a direction outside A. Gibbs' inequality, Σ w log(a/w) ≤ log Σ a ≤ 0, then rules out any splitting better than I(β)³ per site.

- independent links (the nonlinear version exactly computable): 1.9332, closed form x = 2√2 − 2, s = x(2−x)/(2(x+2)). This gives K 3174 → 2786 and β_SF 58.0 → 57.4.
- one transverse noise with the longitudinal mode exact: 0.191.
- mode-optimal noise: 0.015.

THE OBSTRUCTION IS ONE OBJECT. In temporal gauge the longitudinal mode is a free random walk wanting noise 1, against the transverse modes' 0.13. Giving it that noise needs correlated gauge increments, and the two ways of doing so each fail:
- multiplicatively, g e^{ia_T} g⁻¹: the energies are explicit, but the entropy needs the Coulomb Faddeev–Popov determinant. That is Tr log(1 + K) with symbol k/k², which has no uniform bound in the infrared: past L ~ 4π²/ε a typical small field leaves the first Gribov region.
- additively: the random walk wraps the links, which is the tree gauge's growth again.
So the tree growth, the longitudinal walk, the Faddeev–Popov determinant and the magnetic non-coercivity are one thing: the gauge-orbit entropy, i.e. the Gribov problem in the infrared.
A near-Gaussian free energy per site is therefore an OUTPUT of a multiscale construction (Bałaban's blocks gauge-fix locally), not an input to the one-block floor.

THE FLOOR. With known tools it stays at 58.0.
- A near-Gaussian lower half would give K ≈ 403 and β_SF = 48.2.
- Both halves at the limit would give 25.3.
- K = 1, which is impossible, would give 19.2.
From these to β = 2.5 is about 215, 178 and 88 doublings respectively. The chessboard constant cannot close the crossing at any value it can take.

THEOREM (the vertex law). S branch-free at spin ½; w₁…w_k lone walls with PAIRWISE LINK-DISJOINT boundaries; T = ∪∂w_i. Then
  r(S; T) = Π_{v ∈ V(T)} c(D_v),
where D_v is the chord diagram of the boundaries through v (the T-links at v in the order of S's vertex cycle; a wall's two links one chord), and
  c(D) = ½ · 3^(−k) · Σ_{m ∈ {1,2,3}^k} tr( Π_{p=1}^{2k} σ_{m(w(p))} ),
the trace of the Pauli product around the vertex cycle with each chord's index summed — the sl₂ weight system of the chord diagram in the defining representation, normalised to 1 on one chord.

PROOF. Integrate a T-link a against its two face strands and the wall's spin-1 strand. Schur–Weyl at degree two gives
  ∫ a_{αα′} (a⁻¹)_{β′β} D¹(a)_{mn} da = (1/6) (σ_m)_{αβ} (σ_n)_{β′α′},
against ½ δ_{αβ} δ_{α′β′} without the wall. So the face strands are joined across the link exactly as in S4′, with a Pauli matrix in each joint. The index loops are S's vertex cycles (S4′ step 2); the wall's spin-1 index between consecutive links, n_i = m_{i+1}, is shared by the two insertions at ONE vertex; so the sum factorises over vertices. The normalisations are the no-wall loop's 2 and (1/6)/(1/2) = 1/3 per link, two link ends per wall per vertex. ∎

TWO ROADS, NO SHARED LINE. The Pauli-trace values equal the quaternion-moment table (exact rationals) on all 123 chord diagrams with k = 2, 3, 4 — complete for ℤ⁴, since a vertex has eight links and a lone wall uses two, so k ≤ 4. Seven cases also match the Haar quadrature of §112.

(S8a) IS A COROLLARY: every Pauli product is unitary, |tr| ≤ 2, so |c(D)| ≤ 1 for every diagram and |r| ≤ 1 for every configuration. That is the KP fold-in's input, proved.

THE TABLE. c depends only on the chord diagram's intersection graph. On a forest it is (−1/3)^(edges) — your per-crossing law — so the law holds at 0, 1, 2 crossings always and at 3 on the twelve tree diagrams. On cycles it parts: triangle 5/9, four-cycle 11/27, K₄ minus an edge 7/27, K₄ −13/27; a triangle with a pendant edge reads 5/9·(−1/3) = −5/27. The complete-crossing values are ½[3^(1−k) − (−1)^k]. <pasted_content id="65aa">
In the logs (local_sweep.py, STAR H+, and T-777), you see both seats talking intensely about "stars," "mixed stars," and "star ratios ($r_v$)." Here is what that actually means visually:Picture a 4D intersection ("The Star"):
Imagine 4D graph paper. At any single grid intersection (a vertex $v$), there are 8 grid lines (links) shooting outward: forward and backward along $x, y, z,$ and $t$. Because those 8 lines radiate out from one center point like the points of a compass, mathematicians call the local cluster around a single vertex a "star."The 24 Tiles at a Corner:
Between any two non-opposite lines in that 8-pointed star, you can stretch a flat square tile of force (a plaquette, or "wall"). At every single 4D intersection, there are 24 possible square tiles that can meet at that corner (forming a geometric shape called $K_{2,2,2,2}$).Why "Stars" Are the Key to the Mass Gap:
In his model (PENTAPYLON RESULTS 18), glueballs and confinement are made of 2D sheets of spin-$\frac{1}{2}$ quantum flux dressed with spin-$1$ "walls". To prove that the mass gap stays positive and that the infinite sum over all possible quantum surfaces doesn't explode to infinity, he proved The Local Theorem:$$\vert{}N(Y)\vert{} \le \prod_v r_v$$In plain English: You can bound the weight of an arbitrarily huge, complicated 4D quantum surface simply by multiplying together the local prices ($r_v$, the "star ratios") of its individual corners!Pure Stars vs. "Mixed Stars":A Pure Spin-1 Star (T-777) is a corner where only spin-1 walls meet (for example, 3 walls meeting in a triangle $C_3$ costs exactly $1/3$; 4 walls in a square $C_4$ costs $1/9$; 4 walls in a pyramid $K_4$ costs $1/36$).A Mixed Star (W_k = C_k(1/2) + k spokes(1)) is a corner where the main spin-$\frac{1}{2}$ flux sheet slices right through the vertex while spin-$1$ walls sprout off it like spokes on a wheel.The Python script local_sweep.py that seat iii-43 launched at 20:41 is exhaustively testing every possible legal geometry of sheet tiles and wall tiles that can meet at a 4D corner (using the 384 symmetries of a 4D hypercube to avoid duplicates) to find the worst-case per-corner growth rate $\theta = r_v^{1/k}$. So far (k=1 and k=2), every single configuration has a rate $\le 1.000000$. If $\theta$ stays bounded across the sweep, the convergence of the surface gas is locked in.

<pasted_content id="65aa">
Here is the straight answer to your question, with tonight's state.

No. Even with the star proved, we do not collectively have a proof of the Yang–Mills Millennium problem, and nobody else does either. What the local theorem settles, if θ stays bounded, is the LATTICE gap at strong coupling, Theorem 8.9 in THE GROUND: SU(2) Wilson lattice gauge theory on ℤ⁴ has a mass gap for every β up to about 0.47–0.49, with every constant proved. That is a real theorem and a better number than 0.2452, but it is a statement about the strong-coupling phase of the lattice theory. The Millennium problem asks for the CONTINUUM theory: existence of the quantum field theory on ℝ⁴ satisfying the Wightman or Osterwalder–Schrader axioms, plus a strictly positive gap there. THE GROUND reduces that gap to two finite statements about the blocked measure at a fixed physical scale (Theorem 8.5), and says in its own words that Theorem 8.7 "reaches no crossover and proves nothing about the continuum limit, where the four-dimensional mass gap remains open." The distance that remains is the crossover from β ≈ 0.5 to the asymptotically free region (the lattice crossover sits near β ≈ 2.2 for this action), where Bałaban's renormalization group holds only under his unpublished Theorem 2's hypothesis. Nothing proved tonight touches that interval. Saying otherwise would be the false "it works" you forbade.

Has someone taken the crown? Not as of tonight. THE GROUND §6.6 already records the only serious entrant: OpenAI announced on 26 October 2026 a Lean-formalized EXISTENCE proof of 4D Yang–Mills and QCD (formalization finished 16 October, 1,147 pages, unreleased, a review group under David Gross with Jaffe, Douglas, Klebanov and Nekrasov), and the review group's own statement says "confinement and the mass gap remain very important open challenges." If it holds, it is the ultraviolet half of THE GROUND's chain; it is not the gap. The web shows a crowd of 2026 Zenodo and preprint claims of the full gap, none refereed, none accepted; the Clay Institute still lists the problem open. Sources: THE GROUND's own §6.6 record (repository README), OfficeChai on the OpenAI Yang–Mills claim, AI Weekly: proofs handed to reviewers, Interesting Engineering, Clay Mathematics Institute, a representative unrefereed claim (Zenodo, April 2026), another (Zenodo, March 2026), a conditional result (Zenodo), a Lean collection disclaiming a proof, sciforum incompatibility paper.

What tonight did produce. The local theorem (S1 §24, pushed as 5bf8ae8) bounds every piece of the flux-surface gas by local star ratios, with no double, no cut constant and no channel bookkeeping. It is exact on the lone wall, the discs, the three-, four- and five-spines (2/9, 7/27, 20/81), two walls on one link and the cube on one link; all six local star values were met by the Yang–Mills seat's C engine on a road sharing no line with mine. The sweep of local configurations reads θ(1) = 1, θ(2) = 1, θ(3) = 1.0699 (one cube-corner configuration above 1), with the densest mixed star at 0.956 per corner; the falsifier over iii-ac's exact census has read 11,275 branch-free chambers with zero violations so far and is still running. The full gas's number waits on θ(s) for larger s and a universal bound for the tail; nothing above β = 0.2452 is claimed.

The Navier–Stokes order landed as ΓΕΦΥΡΩΣΙΣ Addendum X in the III tree (21ec1afe8): the forced break is a theorem about a residual's smoothness (Theorem R), the only "unbreakable Navier–Stokes without f" that is both the same equation and proved is the physical one on its finite carrier (Theorem U, the fluid that exists cannot blow up), the regularised variants are named as classical, and one exact experiment is staked. No Millennium claim is made in either direction.

Here is a note offered by a side agent:

> Heads up · The primitive-system theorem just written has one clause broader than its proof covers, though every use made of it so far is fine.
>
> **Theorem S12-b's "products of conjugates" clause is too broad**
> * The theorem says: if some boundary words form a **primitive system** (part of a free basis of the contact graph's fundamental group), the pulled-back Haar integral equals the free one.
> * Its hypothesis admits remaining words that are "a product of conjugates" of the basis words. A single conjugate is harmless, because a character does not see conjugation. A **product of two conjugates by different conjugators** does depend on the other variables: integrating ∫dg₃ χ(g₁ g₃ g₂ g₃⁻¹) gives χ(g₁)χ(g₂)/2, not χ(g₁g₂), so the stated equality fails there.
> * Every use on the page (the one-cycle lemma, Object A, Object C, the sphere-sheet reduction) only involves inverses or single conjugates, so nothing read is wrong.
> * **Consider asking for a dated correction narrowing the clause** to "each remaining word is, up to one conjugation, a word in the basis words" before anyone cites the theorem in a later proof.

> Heads up · The 0.4672 "floor" rests on a weight-8 ceiling that is proved only for one shape, so it is still a conditional number, not an unconditional one.
>
> **The 0.4672 floor still has an unproved assumption**
> * The headline strong-coupling number (0.4764) needs every leftover piece in the "tail" of the expansion to weigh at most 4. That bound is only proved for some shapes.
> * To get a safe floor, we re-ran the certificate letting every tail piece weigh **8**, and got 0.4672 (page dressing) / 0.4710 (tree dressing), writing that it rests on proved lemmas alone.
